[S5.SS4]
5.4. The group extension and the action of $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ on $\mathcal{I}(M)$
[S5.SS4.p1]
[S5.SS4.p1.1] We are at the position to complete the proof of Theorem 1.1.
[S5.SS4.p2]
[S5.SS4.p2.1] Let $M$ be a simply connected closed 5-manifold (which is not required to be spin). Let $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))$ be the group of automorphisms of the group $H_{2}(M)$ that preserve the linking form $L_{M}$ and the second Stiefel–Whitney class $w_{2}(M)$ (viewed as a homomorphism $H_{2}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2$ ). There is a homomorphism $\phi:\mathrm{MCG}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))$ given by $[f]\mapsto f_{*}$ , and $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))$ is $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ when $M$ is spin. An immediate consequence of [Bar65, Theorem 2.2] is the following.
[S5.ThmDef8]
Theorem 5.8 (Barden).
[S5.ThmDef8.p1]
[S5.ThmDef8.p1.1] There is a short exact sequence of groups
|
| $1\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}(M)\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))\rightarrow 1.$
|
[S5.SS4.p3]
[S5.SS4.p3.1] The first part of Theorem 1.1 is a special case of this theorem. To study this extension problem the following action of $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))$ on $\mathcal{I}(M)$ is crucial (see for example [Bro82, Chapter IV]). Let $h\in\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))$ be an automorphism of $H_{2}(M)$ , and let $f_{h}$ be an orientation preserving diffeomorphism such that ${f_{h}}_{*}=h$ . The action of $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))$ on $\mathcal{I}(M)$ is defined by
|
| $h\cdot[f]\coloneqq[f_{h}\circ f\circ{f_{h}}^{-1}].$
|
[S5.SS4.p4]
[S5.SS4.p4.1] In the remaining part of this subsection let $M$ be a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold with no 2- and 3-torsion in homology. For such $M$ isotopy invariants of diffeomorphisms in $\mathcal{I}(M)$ are defined, and the action of $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ on $\mathcal{I}(M)$ is thus calculable.
[S5.SS4.p5]
[S5.SS4.p5.1] The Torelli group $\mathcal{I}(M)$ is isomorphic to the bordism group $\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{6}(K(H_{2}(M),2))$ . Recall that in 1.1 we defined the following standard action of $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ on $\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{6}(K(H_{2}(M),2))$ . Given $h\in\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ , let $\delta_{h}:K(H_{2}(M),2)\rightarrow K(H_{2}(M),2)$ be the map inducing $h$ on the second homology, which is unique up to homotopy. Then $[N,\delta_{h}\circ\nu]$ is a well-defined bordism class in $\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{6}(K(H_{2}(M),2))$ and the standard action is defined by $h\cdot[N,\nu]\coloneqq[N,\delta_{h}\circ\nu]$ .
[S5.ThmDef9]
Theorem 5.9.
[S5.ThmDef9.p1]
[S5.ThmDef9.p1.1] Let $M$ be a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold with no 2- and 3-torsion in $H_{2}(M)$ . The action of $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ on $\mathcal{I}(M)$ and the standard action of $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ on $\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{6}(K(H_{2}(M),2))$ coincide via the mapping torus construction $\rho$ .
[S5.SS4.2]
Proof.
[S5.SS4.p6]
[S5.SS4.p6.1] It suffices to show that $\rho(h\cdot[f])=h\cdot\rho([f])$ , namely
|
| $[M_{f_{h}\circ f\circ{f_{h}}^{-1}},\nu_{f_{h}\circ f\circ{f_{h}}^{-1}}]=[M_{f},\delta_{h}\circ\nu_{f}].$
|
[S5.SS4.p6.2] We prove this by calculating their bordism invariants. For brevity we write $h\cdot f$ for $f_{h}\circ f\circ{f_{h}}^{-1}$ in the following. Note that
|
| $\displaystyle M_{f}=\biggl(M\times\Bigl([0,1/5]\sqcup[2/5,3/5]\sqcup[4/5,1]\Bigr)\biggr)\Big/$
| $\displaystyle(x,0)\sim(f(x),1),$
|
|
|
| $\displaystyle(x,1/5)\sim(x,2/5),$
|
|
|
| $\displaystyle(x,3/5)\sim(x,4/5);$
|
|
| $\displaystyle M_{h\cdot f}=\biggl(M\times\Bigl([0,1/5]\sqcup[2/5,3/5]\sqcup[4/5,1]\Bigr)\biggr)\Big/$
| $\displaystyle(x,0)\sim(f(x),1),$
|
|
|
| $\displaystyle({f_{h}}^{-1}(x),1/5)\sim(x,2/5),$
|
|
|
| $\displaystyle({f_{h}}(x),3/5)\sim(x,4/5).$
|
[S5.SS4.p6.3] There is an orientation preserving diffeomorphism
|
| $F=id_{M\times[0,1/5]}\cup(f_{h}\times id_{[2/5,3/5]})\cup id_{M\times[4/5,1]}:M_{f}\rightarrow M_{h\cdot f}.$
|
[S5.SS4.p6.4] Let $i_{f}$ and $i_{h\cdot f}$ be the inclusion of $M=M\times\{\frac{1}{2}\}$ in $M_{f}$ and $M_{h\cdot f}$ respectively. Then we have $F\circ i_{f}=i_{h\cdot f}\circ f_{h}$ . It follows that
|
| ${\nu_{h\cdot f}}_{*}(p_{1}(M_{h\cdot f})\cap[M_{h\cdot f}])=h\circ{\nu_{f}}_{*}(p_{1}(M_{f})\cap[M_{f}])={(\delta_{h}\circ{\nu_{f}})}_{*}(p_{1}(M_{f})\cap[M_{f}])$
|
[S5.SS4.p6.5] and
|
| ${\nu_{h\cdot f}}_{*}([M_{h\cdot f}])={\nu_{h\cdot f}}_{*}F_{*}([M_{f}])=h\circ{\nu_{f}}_{*}([M_{f}])={(\delta_{h}\circ{\nu_{f}})}_{*}([M_{f}]).$
|
[S5.SS4.p6.6] Thus $[M_{h\cdot f},\nu_{h\cdot f}]=[M_{f},\delta_{h}\circ\nu_{f}]$ .
∎
[S5.SS4.p7]
[S5.SS4.p7.1] The proof of Theorem 1.1 is thus completed.
[S5.ThmDef10]
Remark 5.10.
[S5.ThmDef10.p1]
[S5.ThmDef10.p1.1] In [Wal63b, Section 4] Wall gave a complete algebraic description of the linking form $L_{M}$ . Wall’s result reduces the determination of $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ to linear algebra. In general, suppose
|
| $H_{2}(M)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{g}\oplus{(\mathbb{Z}/{p_{1}^{r_{1}}})^{2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{(\mathbb{Z}/{p_{s}^{r_{s}}})^{2}},$
|
[S5.ThmDef10.p1.2] then $L_{M}$ is a skew-symmetric non-singular $\mathbb{Q}/{\mathbb{Z}}$ -valued bilinear form over the finite abelian group ${(\mathbb{Z}/{p_{1}^{r_{1}}})^{2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{(\mathbb{Z}/{p_{s}^{r_{s}}})^{2}}$ which is represented by the block diagonal matrix
|
| $\begin{pmatrix}0&\frac{1}{{p_{1}^{r_{1}}}}&&&\\
-\frac{1}{{p_{1}^{r_{1}}}}&0&&&\\
&&\ddots&&\\
&&&0&\frac{1}{{p_{s}^{r_{s}}}}\\
&&&-\frac{1}{{p_{s}^{r_{s}}}}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}$
|
[S5.ThmDef10.p1.3] under the standard basis of ${(\mathbb{Z}/{p_{1}^{r_{1}}})^{2}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{(\mathbb{Z}/{p_{s}^{r_{s}}})^{2}}$ . Then $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ is the group of automorphisms of the abelian group $H_{2}(M)$ preserving this matrix. For instance, when $H_{2}(M)\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/{p^{r}})}^{2}$ , the group $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$ is isomorphic to $\mathrm{SL}_{2}(\mathbb{Z}/{p^{r}})$ .
[S5.SS5]
5.5. Diffeomorphisms acting nontrivially on homology are not Dehn twists
[S5.SS5.p1]
[S5.SS5.p1.1] Let $M$ be a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold with no 2-torsion in homology. We show that a diffeomorphism of $M$ acting nontrivially on $H_{*}(M)$ is not isotopic to any Dehn twist. This is achieved by determining all possible Dehn twists of $M$ .
[S5.SS5.p2]
[S5.SS5.p2.1] The normal bundle of an embedding $S^{k}\hookrightarrow M$ is classified by its clutching function which is an element of $\pi_{k-1}(\mathrm{SO}(5-k))$ , so it is trivial (when $k=2$ , use the fact that $M$ is spin). Thus for $1\leq k\leq 4$ , $S^{k}\times D^{5-k}$ embeds in $M$ if $S^{k}$ embeds in $M$ . Assume that this is the case and let $S:S^{k}\times D^{5-k}\hookrightarrow M$ be an embedding. To define a Dehn twist about $S$ one needs to choose an element of $\pi_{5-k}(\mathrm{SO}(k+1))$ , which is nontrivial only if $k=2,4$ ( $\pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(3))\cong\mathbb{Z}$ and $\pi_{1}(\mathrm{SO}(5))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2$ ), so we have proved the following.
[S5.ThmDef11]
Proposition 5.11.
[S5.ThmDef11.p1]
[S5.ThmDef11.p1.1] A Dehn twist about an embedded 1-sphere or 3-sphere of a closed 5-manifold is isotopic to the identity.
[S5.SS5.p3]
[S5.SS5.p3.1] By Lemma 4.1 a Dehn twist about an embedded 2-sphere lies in the Torelli group of $M$ . It remains to consider Dehn twists about embedded 4-spheres, which are of order 1 or 2 in $\mathrm{MCG}(M)$ since $\pi_{1}(\mathrm{SO}(5))\cong\mathbb{Z}/2$ .
[S5.SS5.p4]
[S5.SS5.p4.1] Let $k=4$ , then $S$ is an embedding of $S^{4}\times D^{1}$ in $M$ . A natural way to produce such an embedding is to realize it as the thickened meridian sphere of a 0-surgery (for example, the “neck” of a connected sum). In fact an embedding of $S^{4}$ in $M$ always arises in this way.
[S5.ThmDef12]
Proposition 5.12.
[S5.ThmDef12.p1]
[S5.ThmDef12.p1.1] Let $M$ be a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold, and let $S:S^{4}\hookrightarrow M$ be an embedding. Then there are two simply connected closed spin 5-manifolds, such that $M$ is the connected sum of them, and $S(S^{4})$ is the meridian sphere of this connected sum operation. In particular, if $H_{2}(M)$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$ or ${(\mathbb{Z}/{p^{r}})}^{2}$ , where $p$ is a prime number, then $S(S^{4})$ is the boundary of an embedded 5-disc.
[S5.SS5.2]
Proof.
[S5.SS5.p5]
[S5.SS5.p5.1] Since $\pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(1))=0$ we may extend $S$ to an embedding $S^{4}\times D^{1}\hookrightarrow M$ . We perform a surgery on this embedding and denote the resulting manifold by $M^{\prime}$ . The homology groups of $M^{\prime}$ can be calculated using homology exact sequence of pairs and are as follows.
[S5.SS5.tab1.1.1]
$i$
$0$
$1$
$2$
$3$
$4$
$5$
[S5.SS5.tab1.1.2]
$H_{i}(M^{\prime})$
| $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$
| $0$
| $H_{2}(M)$
| $H_{3}(M)$
| $0$
| $\mathbb{Z}^{2}$
[S5.SS5.p6]
[S5.SS5.p6.1] Thus $M^{\prime}$ has two path components. By Seifert–van Kampen theorem each component is a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold. Now $M$ is the connected sum of these two manifolds and $S(S^{4})$ is the meridian sphere of this connected sum operation. If $H_{2}(M)$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$ or ${(\mathbb{Z}/{p^{r}})}^{2}$ , by the classification of simply connected 5-manifolds [Sma62, Bar65], $M^{\prime}$ is diffeomorphic to $M\sqcup S^{5}$ .
∎
[S5.ThmDef13]
Corollary 5.13.
[S5.ThmDef13.p1]
[S5.ThmDef13.p1.1] Only the trivial element of $\pi_{4}(S^{2}\times S^{3})\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2}$ can be represented by an embedding of $S^{4}$ .
[S5.ThmDef14]
Corollary 5.14.
[S5.ThmDef14.p1]
[S5.ThmDef14.p1.1] Let $M$ be a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold, and let $S:S^{4}\times D^{1}\hookrightarrow M$ be an embedding.
(1)
[S5.I2.ix1.p1]
[S5.I2.ix1.p1.1] The Dehn twist about $S$ acts trivially on $H_{*}(M)$ .
(2)
[S5.I2.ix2.p1]
[S5.I2.ix2.p1.1] If $H_{2}(M)$ has no 2-torsion, then the Dehn twist about $S$ is isotopic to $id_{M}$ .
(3)
[S5.I2.ix3.p1]
[S5.I2.ix3.p1.1] If $H_{2}(M)$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}$ or ${(\mathbb{Z}/{p^{r}})}^{2}$ , where $p$ is a prime number, then the Dehn twist about $S$ is isotopic to $id_{M}$ .
[S5.SS5.3]
Proof.
[S5.SS5.p7]
[S5.SS5.p7.1] (1) and (2) are clear. To prove (3), note that this Dehn twist is supported in an embedded 5-disc, and thus its isotopy class lies in the image of $\mathrm{MCG}(D^{5},\partial)\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}(M)$ , where $\mathrm{MCG}(D^{5},\partial)=\Theta_{6}=0$ .
∎
[S5.ThmDef15]
Corollary 5.15.
[S5.ThmDef15.p1]
[S5.ThmDef15.p1.1] All the Dehn twists of a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold $M$ act trivially on $H_{*}(M)$ . If $H_{2}(M)$ has no 2-torsion or is isomorphic to ${(\mathbb{Z}/{2^{r}})}^{2}$ , then Dehn twists about embedded $k$ -spheres with $k\neq 2$ are isotopic to $id_{M}$ .
[S5.SS5.p8]
[S5.SS5.p8.1] We have seen in 1.1 that the isotopy classes of Dehn twists about embedded 2-spheres are determined by elements of $\pi_{2}(M)$ and $\pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(3))$ . Thus we have determined all possible Dehn twists of $M$ when $H_{2}(M)$ has no 2-torsion or is isomorphic to ${(\mathbb{Z}/{2^{r}})}^{2}$ .
[S5.ThmDef16]
Remark 5.16.
[S5.ThmDef16.p1]
[S5.ThmDef16.p1.1] The results in this subsection are formulated for spin manifolds. They remain valid in the nonspin case after mild modification of the statements.
[S6]
6. The mapping class group of $M_{g}$ and its abelianization
[S6.p1]
[S6.p1.1] In this section we focus on $M_{g}$ . We show that $\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})$ is a semi-direct product of $\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ and $\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ , and compute the abelianization of $\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})$ . We also determine $\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ , the full mapping class group of $S^{2}\times S^{3}$ .
[S6.SS1]
6.1. Splitting the group extension for $M_{g}$
[S6.SS1.p1]
[S6.SS1.p1.1] Recall that, given a short exact sequence of groups
|
| $0\rightarrow A\rightarrow E\xrightarrow{\phi}Q\rightarrow 1$
|
[S6.SS1.p1.2] with $A$ abelian, the group $Q$ acts on $A$ by $g\cdot a=s(g)as(g)^{-1}$ , where $s:Q\rightarrow E$ is a set theoretic cross section. This action is independent to the choice of $s$ and is used to define the group structure of the semi-direct product $A\rtimes Q$ (see [Bro82, Chapter IV]). We note the following.
[S6.ThmDef1]
Lemma 6.1.
[S6.ThmDef1.p1]
[S6.ThmDef1.p1.1] If there is a map $\chi:E\rightarrow A$ which restricts to identity on $A$ , and
|
| $\chi(g_{1}g_{2})=\chi(g_{1})+g_{1}\chi(g_{2})g_{1}^{-1}$
|
[S6.ThmDef1.p1.2] for $g_{1},g_{2}\in E$ , then the short exact sequence splits.
[S6.SS1.2]
Proof.
[S6.SS1.p2]
[S6.SS1.p2.1] The map $(\chi,\phi):E\rightarrow A\rtimes Q$ is a homomorphism which fits into the following commutative diagram
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\rtimes Q}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Q}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\chi,\phi)}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$
|
[S6.SS1.p2.2] and therefore is an isomorphism.
∎
[S6.SS1.p3]
[S6.SS1.p3.1] We call such a map $\chi$ a crossed splitting.
[S6.SS1.p4]
[S6.SS1.p4.1] For $M=M_{g}$ the short exact sequence in Theorem 5.8 takes the form
|
| $0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 1$
|
[S6.SS1.p4.2] if we identify $H_{2}(M_{g})$ with $\mathbb{Z}^{g}$ via the standard basis ${S_{k}}_{*}([S^{2}])$ . The action of $\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ on $\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ is described by Theorem 5.9. We now construct a crossed splitting $\chi:\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ . We identify $\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ with ${\Omega}^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{6}((\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})^{g})$ via the mapping torus construction $\rho$ .
[S6.SS1.p5]
[S6.SS1.p5.1] Let $f$ be an orientation preserving diffeomorphism of $M_{g}$ . Consider the twisted double
|
| $X_{f}\coloneqq{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\cup_{f}\overline{{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})}.$
|
[S6.SS1.p5.2] This is a simply connected closed oriented 6-manifold. Using the homology exact sequence we see that the inclusion maps induce isomorphisms $H_{2}(M_{g})\cong H_{2}({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))\cong H_{2}(X_{f})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{g}$ . This implies that $X_{f}$ is a spin manifold with unique spin structure, and we could find a map $\tilde{\nu}_{f}:X_{f}\rightarrow{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}$ such that the diagram
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}}({X}_{f})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}}({(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}}({M}_{g})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{H_{2}}({({M}_{g})}_{f})}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\nu}_{f*}}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}\circ f_{*}}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\nu}_{f*}}$
|
[S6.SS1.p5.3] commutes, where the left vertical map is induced by the inclusion map which factors through the inclusion $M_{g}=\partial({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))\hookrightarrow{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})$ into the first copy of ${\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})$ . This determines a bordism class $[X_{f},\tilde{\nu}_{f}]\in{\Omega}^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{6}((\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})^{g})$ . The map
|
| $\displaystyle\chi:\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})$
| $\displaystyle\rightarrow{\Omega}^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{6}((\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})^{g})$
|
|
| $\displaystyle[f]$
| $\displaystyle\mapsto[X_{f},\tilde{\nu}_{f}]$
|
[S6.SS1.p5.4] is clearly well-defined.
[S6.ThmDef2]
Lemma 6.2.
[S6.ThmDef2.p1]
[S6.ThmDef2.p1.1] If $[f]\in\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ , then $\chi([f])=\rho([f])$ .
[S6.SS1.3]
Proof.
[S6.SS1.p6]
[S6.SS1.p6.1] Let $B\coloneqq{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}\times B\mathrm{Spin}$ . It suffices to construct a normal $B$ -bordism between $(X_{f},\tilde{\nu}_{f})$ and $({(M_{g})}_{f},\nu_{f})$ . Choose a collar neighborhood $M_{g}\times[0,1]\subset{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})$ . The manifold
|
| $V\coloneqq{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times I/{\sim},$
|
[S6.SS1.p6.2] obtained by identifying $M_{g}\times[0,1]\times\{0\}$ with $M_{g}\times[0,1]\times\{1\}$ via the gluing map $f\times id_{[0,1]}:M_{g}\times[0,1]\times\{0\}\rightarrow M_{g}\times[0,1]\times\{1\}$ , is an oriented bordism between $X_{f}$ and ${(M_{g})}_{f}$ . We show that $V$ can be made into a normal $B$ -bordism. Using Mayer–Vietoris sequences we see that $V$ is a spin manifold having two spin structures which restrict to the two spin structures of ${(M_{g})}_{f}$ respectively. Since $X_{f}$ has a unique spin structure, we can choose the spin structure of $V$ such that $V$ is a spin bordism. Moreover, from Mayer–Vietoris sequences we see that the inclusion maps $X_{f}\hookrightarrow V$ and ${(M_{g})}_{f}\hookrightarrow V$ induce isomorphisms
|
| $H_{2}(X_{f})\cong H_{2}(V)\cong H_{2}({(M_{g})}_{f}),$
|
[S6.SS1.p6.3] consequently by universal coefficient theorem there are isomorphisms
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2}(X_{f}),\mathbb{Z}^{g})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2}(V),\mathbb{Z}^{g})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathbb{Z}}(H_{2}({(M_{g})}_{f}),\mathbb{Z}^{g})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[{X_{f}},{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}]}}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[V,{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}]}}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[{{(M_{g})}_{f}},{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}]}.}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}$
|
[S6.SS1.p6.4] Since $f$ lies in the Torelli group, by our definition of $\tilde{\nu}_{f}$ there is a map $\overline{\nu}_{f}:V\rightarrow{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}$ extends $\tilde{\nu}_{f}\sqcup\nu_{f}$ up to homotopy. By gluing collar neighborhoods to $V$ if needed, we obtain the desired normal $B$ -bordism between $(X_{f},\tilde{\nu}_{f})$ and $({(M_{g})}_{f},\nu_{f})$ .
∎
[S6.ThmDef3]
Lemma 6.3.
[S6.ThmDef3.p1]
[S6.ThmDef3.p1.1] The map $\chi:\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})\rightarrow{\Omega}^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{6}((\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})^{g})$ is a crossed splitting.
[S6.SS1.4]
Proof.
[S6.SS1.p7]
[S6.SS1.p7.1] By Lemma 6.2 it suffices to show that $\chi([f\circ f^{\prime}])=\chi([f])+[f\circ\chi([f^{\prime}])\circ f^{-1}]$ . Namely, let $h\coloneqq f_{*}\in\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ , let $\delta_{h}:(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})^{g}\rightarrow(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})^{g}$ be the map inducing $h$ on $\mathbb{Z}^{g}$ , we show that
|
| $[X_{f\circ f^{\prime}},\tilde{\nu}_{f\circ f^{\prime}}]=[X_{f},\tilde{\nu}_{f}]+[X_{f^{\prime}},\delta_{h}\circ\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}}].$
|
[S6.SS1.p8]
[S6.SS1.p8.1] We construct a spin bordism $Y$ between $X_{f}\sqcup X_{f^{\prime}}$ and $X_{f\circ f^{\prime}}$ as follows. Let
|
|
| $\displaystyle Y_{f^{\prime}}\coloneqq\bigl({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times[0,2]\bigr)\cup_{f^{\prime}\times id_{[0,1]}}\bigl({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times[0,1]\bigr),$
|
|
|
| $\displaystyle Y_{f}\coloneqq\bigl({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times[0,1]\bigr)\cup_{f\times id_{[0,1]}}\bigl({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times[0,2]\bigr).$
|
[S6.SS1.p8.2] Let $Z$ be the manifold obtained by gluing $M_{g}\times[1,2]$ and ${\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})$ via the identification $M_{g}\times\{1\}=\partial({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))$ . Clearly $Z$ is diffeomorphic to ${\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})$ . Let
|
| $Y\coloneqq Y_{f^{\prime}}\cup_{F^{\prime}}(Z\times I)\cup_{F}Y_{f}$
|
[S6.SS1.p8.3] where $F^{\prime}$ is the map from $(\partial({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))\times[1,2])\cup_{f^{\prime}}({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times\{1\})\subset\partial Y_{f^{\prime}}$ to $Z\times\{0\}$ given by the union of
|
| $\displaystyle f^{\prime}\times id_{[1,2]}$
| $\displaystyle:\partial({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))\times[1,2]\rightarrow M_{g}\times[1,2]\times\{0\},$
|
|
| $\displaystyle id$
| $\displaystyle:{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times\{1\}\rightarrow{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times\{0\},$
|
[S6.SS1.p8.4] and $F$ is the map from $Z\times\{1\}$ to $({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times\{1\})\cup_{f}(\partial({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))\times[1,2])\subset\partial Y_{f}$ given by the union of
|
| $\displaystyle f\times id_{[1,2]}$
| $\displaystyle:M_{g}\times[1,2]\times\{1\}\rightarrow\partial({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))\times[1,2],$
|
|
| $\displaystyle id$
| $\displaystyle:{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times\{1\}\rightarrow{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times\{1\}.$
|
[S6.SS1.p8.5] Clearly $Y$ is a simply connected bordism between $X_{f}\sqcup X_{f^{\prime}}$ and $X_{f\circ f^{\prime}}$ . Using Mayer–Vietoris sequence we see that $Y$ is spin. Then the spin structure of $Y$ is unique and thus $Y$ is a spin bordism.
[S6.SS1.p9]
[S6.SS1.p9.1] We shall construct a map $\tilde{\nu}:Y\rightarrow{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}$ extending $\tilde{\nu}_{f\circ f^{\prime}}\sqcup\tilde{\nu}_{f}\sqcup\delta_{h}\circ\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}}$ , thus making $(Y,\tilde{\nu})$ into a normal $B$ -bordism (where $B\coloneqq{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}\times B\mathrm{Spin}$ ) and as a consequence $[X_{f\circ f^{\prime}},\tilde{\nu}_{f\circ f^{\prime}}]=[X_{f},\tilde{\nu}_{f}]+[X_{f^{\prime}},\delta_{h}\circ\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}}]$ . Denote the restriction of $\tilde{\nu}_{f}$ on the first and on the second copy of ${\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})$ by ${(\tilde{\nu}_{f})}_{1}$ and ${(\tilde{\nu}_{f})}_{2}$ respectively. Similar notations apply to $\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}}$ and $\tilde{\nu}_{f\circ f^{\prime}}$ . Note that by our definition of $\tilde{\nu}_{f}$ we have
|
| $\delta_{h}\circ{{(\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}})}_{2}}_{*}={{(\tilde{\nu}_{f})}_{1}}_{*}:H_{2}({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))\rightarrow H_{2}({(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}),$
|
[S6.SS1.p9.2] thus $\delta_{h}\circ{(\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}})}_{2}$ is homotopic to ${(\tilde{\nu}_{f})}_{1}$ . Choose a homotopy $H:{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\times I\rightarrow{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}$ from $\delta_{h}\circ{(\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}})}_{2}$ to ${(\tilde{\nu}_{f})}_{1}$ , and denote the restriction of $H$ on $\partial({\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4}))\times I$ by $\partial H$ . The map $\tilde{\nu}:Y\rightarrow{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}$ is defined as the union of the following maps
|
| $\displaystyle\bigl((\delta_{h}\circ{(\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}})}_{1})\times[0,2]\bigr)\cup\bigl((\delta_{h}\circ{(\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}})}_{2})\times[0,1]\bigr)$
| $\displaystyle:Y_{f^{\prime}}\rightarrow{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g},$
|
|
| $\displaystyle H\cup\bigl(\partial H\times[1,2]\bigr)$
| $\displaystyle:Z\times I\rightarrow{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g},$
|
|
| $\displaystyle\bigl({(\tilde{\nu}_{f})}_{1}\times[0,1]\bigr)\cup\bigl({(\tilde{\nu}_{f})}_{2}\times[0,2]\bigr)$
| $\displaystyle:Y_{f}\rightarrow{(\mathbb{CP}^{\infty})}^{g}.$
|
[S6.SS1.p9.3] Since the effects of $\delta_{h}\circ{(\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}})}_{1}$ and ${(\tilde{\nu}_{f\circ f^{\prime}})}_{1}$ on the second homology groups coincide, $(X_{f\circ f^{\prime}},\tilde{\nu}_{f\circ f^{\prime}})$ and $(X_{f\circ f^{\prime}},\tilde{\nu})$ are normal $B$ -bordant, so $(Y,\tilde{\nu})$ can be viewed as a normal $B$ -bordism between $(X_{f\circ f^{\prime}},\tilde{\nu}_{f\circ f^{\prime}})$ and $(X_{f},\tilde{\nu}_{f})\sqcup(X_{f^{\prime}},\delta_{h}\circ\tilde{\nu}_{f^{\prime}})$ . This proves the lemma.
∎
[S6.SS1.p10]
[S6.SS1.p10.1] Therefore $\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})\cong\mathcal{I}(M_{g})\rtimes\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ . Moreover, let $\mathrm{Top}^{+}(M)$ be the topological group of orientation preserving homeomorphisms of an oriented manifold $M$ , and let $G^{+}(M)$ be the topological monoid of orientation preserving homotopy equivalences of $M$ , both equipped with compact-open topology. The topological mapping class group of $M$ ,
|
| $\mathrm{MCG}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M)\coloneqq\pi_{0}\mathrm{Top}^{+}(M),$
|
[S6.SS1.p10.2] is the group of topological isotopy classes of orientation preserving homeomorphisms of $M$ , and the homotopy automorphism group of $M$ ,
|
| $\mathrm{haut}(M)\coloneqq\pi_{0}G^{+}(M),$
|
[S6.SS1.p10.3] is the group of homotopy classes of orientation preserving homotopy equivalences of $M$ . Recall that we have defined in 1.1 the homotopy Torelli group $\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)$ of $M$ consisting of homotopy classes of homotopy equivalences acting trivially on $H_{*}(M)$ . Similarly the topological Torelli group $\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M)$ of $M$ is the subgroup of $\mathrm{MCG}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M)$ consisting of homeomorphisms acting trivially on $H_{*}(M)$ . If $M$ is a simply connected closed 5-manifold, Barden’s result [Bar65, Theorem 2.2] implies that there is a commutative diagram of short exact sequences
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MCG}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MCG}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{haut}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M},w_{2}(M))}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$
|
[S6.SS1.p10.4] where the vertical maps are forgetful homomorphisms. Combining this with Lemma 6.1 and Lemma 6.3 we have the following.
[S6.ThmDef4]
Theorem 6.4.
[S6.ThmDef4.p1]
[S6.ThmDef4.p1.1] There are splitting short exact sequences
|
| $0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 1,$
|
|
| $1\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 1,$
|
|
| $1\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{h}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{haut}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 1.$
|
[S6.SS1.p11]
[S6.SS1.p11.1] Here for a short exact sequence
|
| $0\rightarrow A\rightarrow E\xrightarrow{\phi}Q\rightarrow 1$
|
[S6.SS1.p11.2] with $A$ not necessarily an abelian group, we say that this short exact sequence splits if there is a group theoretic cross section, namely a homomorphism $s:Q\rightarrow E$ such that $\phi\circ s=id_{Q}$ .
[S6.SS1.p12]
[S6.SS1.p12.1] By Theorem 6.4 the group structure of $\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})$ is determined. This completes the proof of Theorem 1.2.
[S6.ThmDef5]
Remark 6.5.
[S6.ThmDef5.p1]
[S6.ThmDef5.p1.1] The determination of the group structures of $\mathrm{MCG}^{\mathrm{Top}}(M_{g})$ and $\mathrm{haut}(M_{g})$ leaves open. (However, see [BB96] for various results on $\mathrm{haut}(M_{g})$ .)
[S6.ThmDef6]
Remark 6.6.
[S6.ThmDef6.p1]
[S6.ThmDef6.p1.1] Given an orientation preserving diffeomorphism $f$ of $M_{g}$ one may also consider the simply connected closed spin 6-manifolds
|
| ${\natural}^{g}(S^{3}\times D^{3})\cup_{f}\overline{{\natural}^{g}(S^{3}\times D^{3})},\quad{\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{4})\cup_{f}\overline{{\natural}^{g}(S^{3}\times D^{3})}.$
|
[S6.ThmDef6.p1.2] By computing their homology groups and applying [Wal66, Theorem 1] and [KM63], one sees that the first manifold is diffeomorphic to ${\#}^{g}(S^{3}\times S^{3})$ , and the second manifold is diffeomorphic to $S^{6}$ .
[S6.SS2]
6.2. The abelianization of $\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})$
[S6.SS2.p1]
[S6.SS2.p1.1] The Hochschild–Serre spectral sequence of the exact sequence
|
| $0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(M_{g})\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})\xrightarrow{\phi}\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 1$
|
[S6.SS2.p1.2] with trivial coefficient module $\mathbb{Z}$ yields the following exact sequence
|
| $H_{2}(\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g}))\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}H_{2}(\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z}))\rightarrow{\mathcal{I}(M_{g})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})}\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g}))\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}H_{1}(\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z}))\rightarrow 0.$
|
[S6.SS2.p1.3] Here ${\mathcal{I}(M_{g})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})}$ denotes the coinvariant, which is the quotient group of $\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ modulo the additive subgroup generated by
|
| $\{h\cdot[f]-[f]\;|\;h\in\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z}),[f]\in\mathcal{I}(M_{g})\}.$
|
[S6.SS2.p1.4] It follows from Theorem 6.4 that $\phi_{*}:H_{2}(\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g}))\rightarrow H_{2}(\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z}))$ is surjective, and there is a splitting short exact sequence
|
| $0\rightarrow{\mathcal{I}(M_{g})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})}\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g}))\xrightarrow{\phi_{*}}H_{1}(\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z}))\rightarrow 0.$
|
[S6.SS2.p1.5] It is a well-known fact that
|
| $H_{1}(\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z}))\cong\begin{cases}{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2},&g=2;\\
\mathbb{Z}/2,&g\neq 2.\end{cases}$
|
[S6.SS2.p1.6] Thus, to compute the abelianization $H_{1}(\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g}))$ of $\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})$ , it suffices to compute the coinvariant ${\mathcal{I}(M_{g})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})}$ .
[S6.ThmDef7]
Lemma 6.7.
[S6.ThmDef7.p1]
[S6.ThmDef7.p1.1] We have
|
| ${\mathcal{I}(M_{g})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})}\cong\begin{cases}{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2},&g=1;\\
\mathbb{Z}/2,&g=2,3;\\
0,&g\geq 4.\end{cases}$
|
[S6.SS2.2]
Proof.
[S6.SS2.p2]
[S6.SS2.p2.1] The proof is a straightforward calculation using the action of $\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ on $\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ determined in Theorem 5.9. For $g=1$ the result is clear. For $g\geq 2$ , since $\mathcal{I}(M_{g})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{g}\oplus H_{g}$ and $\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ acts on the $\mathbb{Z}^{g}$ summand in the standard way (this follows from the definition of the bordism invariants), we have ${\mathcal{I}(M_{g})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})}={(H_{g})}_{\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})}$ . Recall that the general linear group $\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ is generated by the (block) diagonal matrices (where the empty entries are 1 on the diagonal and 0 otherwise)
|
| $\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0&&\\
0&1&0&&\\
0&0&1&&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&1\\
\end{pmatrix},\;\begin{pmatrix}-1&0&0&&\\
0&1&0&&\\
0&0&1&&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&1\\
\end{pmatrix},\;\begin{pmatrix}0&1&0&&\\
1&0&0&&\\
0&0&1&&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&1\\
\end{pmatrix},\cdots,\begin{pmatrix}1&&&&\\
&\ddots&&&\\
&&1&0&0\\
&&0&0&1\\
&&0&1&0\\
\end{pmatrix}.$
|
[S6.SS2.p2.2] It suffices to check the action of these matrices on the generators of $H_{g}$ , which can be calculated using the universal coefficient theorem.
[S6.SS2.p3]
[S6.SS2.p3.1] For $g=2,3$ we see that the generators $2(x_{k}^{2}x_{l})^{*}$ are zero in the coinvariant, and the remaining generators are mapped to the same element, which is of order at most 2, in the coinvariant. Thus the coinvariant for $g=2,3$ is $0$ or $\mathbb{Z}/2$ . Consider the homomorphism $H_{g}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2$ defined by adding the coordinates except those correspond to the generators $2(x_{k}^{2}x_{l})^{*}$ . By checking on the generators of $\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ we see that this is a surjective homomorphism which is invariant under the action of $\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ , thus it induces a surjection from the coinvariant to $\mathbb{Z}/2$ , it follows that the coinvariant is $\mathbb{Z}/2$ for $g=2,3$ . For $g\geq 4$ the aforementioned effects on the generators of $H_{g}$ are still true, namely, $2(x_{k}^{2}x_{l})^{*}$ are zero in the coinvariant, and the remaining generators (including $(x_{k}x_{l}x_{h})^{*}$ ) are mapped to the same element, which is of order at most 2, in the coinvariant. Moreover, the generators $(x_{k}x_{l}x_{h})^{*}$ are mapped to zero, which force all generators of $H_{g}$ to be zero in the coinvariant. Thus the coinvariant is trivial for $g\geq 4$ .
∎
[S6.SS2.3]
Proof of Corollary 1.6.
[S6.SS2.p4]
[S6.SS2.p4.1] Part (1) is evident. Part (2) follows from the well-known fact that given a topological group $G$ , one has $\pi_{1}(BG)\cong\pi_{0}(G)$ . Therefore $H_{1}(BG)\cong H_{1}(\pi_{0}(G))$ .
∎
[S6.SS3]
6.3. The full mapping class group of $S^{2}\times S^{3}$
[S6.SS3.p1]
[S6.SS3.p1.1] Barden’s theorem [Bar65, Theorem 2.2] implies that for each simply connected closed 5-manifold $M$ there is an exact sequence
|
| $1\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\rightarrow 1,$
|
[S6.SS3.p1.2] where $\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(M)\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2$ maps an isotopy class to its degree. Whether this exact sequence splits or not is unknown in general. For $M=S^{2}\times S^{3}$ a (group theoretic) cross section $\mathbb{Z}/2\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ is provided by the orientation reversing diffeomorphism $r:S^{2}\times S^{3}\rightarrow S^{2}\times S^{3}$ defined as $(x,y)\mapsto(-x,y)$ , where $-1$ denotes the antipodal map. As a generalization of Corollary 1.3 we have the following computation of $\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ .
[S6.ThmDef8]
Theorem 6.8.
[S6.ThmDef8.p1]
[S6.ThmDef8.p1.1] The full mapping class group $\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ has the following presentation:
|
| $\left\langle a,b,v,w|ab=ba,v^{2}=1,vav=a^{-1},vbv=b^{-1},w^{2}=1,vw=wv,aw=wa,bw=wb\right\rangle,$
|
[S6.ThmDef8.p1.2] where $a$ , $b$ , $v$ , $w$ can be represented by $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}$ , $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1}\#S_{1},\alpha}$ , $R$ and $r$ respectively.
[S6.SS3.2]
Proof.
[S6.SS3.p2]
[S6.SS3.p2.1] We only need to prove that, given a diffeomorphism $f$ whose isotopy class lies in the Torelli group $\mathcal{I}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ , we have $r\circ f\circ r$ isotopic to $f$ . The proof is similar to that of Theorem 5.9. In the following we write $r\cdot f$ for $r\circ f\circ r$ . Note that
|
| $\displaystyle(S^{2}\times S^{3})_{f}=\bigl((S^{2}\times S^{3}\times I)\sqcup(S^{2}\times S^{3})\bigr)\Big/$
| $\displaystyle(x,0)\sim f(x),$
|
|
|
| $\displaystyle(x,1)\sim x;$
|
|
| $\displaystyle(S^{2}\times S^{3})_{r\cdot f}=\bigl((S^{2}\times S^{3}\times I)\sqcup(S^{2}\times S^{3})\bigr)\Big/$
| $\displaystyle(x,0)\sim f\circ r(x),$
|
|
|
| $\displaystyle(x,1)\sim r(x).$
|
[S6.SS3.p2.2] There is an orientation reversing diffeomorphism
|
| $F=(r\times id_{I})\cup id_{S^{2}\times S^{3}}:(S^{2}\times S^{3})_{f}\rightarrow(S^{2}\times S^{3})_{r\cdot f}.$
|
[S6.SS3.p2.3] We have $F_{*}([(S^{2}\times S^{3})_{f}])=-[(S^{2}\times S^{3})_{r\cdot f}]$ and $p_{1}((S^{2}\times S^{3})_{f})=F^{*}(p_{1}((S^{2}\times S^{3})_{r\cdot f}))$ . By calculating the bordism invariants from these facts we see that $r\cdot f$ is isotopic to $f$ .
∎
[S6.SS3.p3]
[S6.SS3.p3.1] Using the geometric interpretation of the Hopf invariant, one easily calculates the induced homomorphisms of $R$ and $r$ on $\pi_{3}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ , and obtain that $R_{*}([S_{1}\circ\eta])=[S_{1}\circ\eta]$ and $R_{*}([T_{1}])=-[T_{1}]$ , and $r_{*}=id$ . Thus the action of $\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ on $\pi_{3}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ is completely determined. This will be used in Section 9.
[S7]
7. Stabilization of $\mathrm{MCG}(M)$
[S7.p1]
[S7.p1.1] In this section $M$ denotes a simply connected closed spin manifold with no 2-torsion in homology. We study the stabilization of $\mathrm{MCG}(M)$ with respect to connected sum with $S^{2}\times S^{3}$ . Let $D\subset M$ be an embedded 5-disc, and let $W\coloneqq M\setminus\mathring{D}$ be the compact 5-manifold obtained by digging out the interior of $D$ from $M$ . Corollary 3.4 implies that
|
| $\mathrm{MCG}(M)=\mathrm{MCG}(M,D)=\mathrm{MCG}(W,\partial)$
|
[S7.p1.2] and
|
| $\mathcal{I}(M)=\mathcal{I}(M,D)=\mathcal{I}(W,\partial).$
|
[S7.p1.3] Fix two disjoint embedded 5-discs $D_{1},D_{2}\subset S^{2}\times S^{3}$ . The manifold $(S^{2}\times S^{3})\setminus(\mathring{D_{1}}\sqcup\mathring{D_{2}})$ has two boundary components, each of which is diffeomorphic to $S^{4}$ . Identify one of the boundary components of $(S^{2}\times S^{3})\setminus(\mathring{D_{1}}\sqcup\mathring{D_{2}})$ with the boundary of $W$ , and glue $(S^{2}\times S^{3})\setminus(\mathring{D_{1}}\sqcup\mathring{D_{2}})$ with $W$ via this identification, we obtain a manifold diffeomorphic to $(M\#(S^{2}\times S^{3}))\setminus\mathring{D}$ . Let $f$ be a diffeomorphism in $\mathrm{MCG}(W,\partial)$ , extending $f$ to $(M\#(S^{2}\times S^{3}))\setminus\mathring{D}$ by $id_{(S^{2}\times S^{3})\setminus(\mathring{D_{1}}\sqcup\mathring{D_{2}})}$ yields a diffeomorphism in $\mathrm{MCG}((M\#(S^{2}\times S^{3}))\setminus\mathring{D},\partial)$ . This induces a well-defined homomorphism
|
| $\mathrm{MCG}(W,\partial)\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}((M\#(S^{2}\times S^{3}))\setminus\mathring{D},\partial),$
|
[S7.p1.4] which can be viewed as a homomorphism
|
| $\mathrm{MCG}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}((M\#(S^{2}\times S^{3})))$
|
[S7.p1.5] mapping an isotopy class $[f]$ to the well-defined isotopy class $[f\#id_{S^{2}\times S^{3}}]$ , independent to the choice of $D\subset M$ by the disc theorem. Clearly it restricts to a homomorphism
|
| $\mathcal{I}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(M\#(S^{2}\times S^{3})).$
|
[S7.p1.6] We call these homomorphisms the stabilization maps. Two orientation preserving diffeomorphisms of $M$ are stably isotopic if their isotopy classes are mapped to the same element by a composition of stabilization maps. Denote $\mathrm{MCG}(M\#M_{g})$ and $\mathcal{I}(M\#M_{g})$ by $\mathrm{MCG}_{g}(M)$ and $\mathcal{I}_{g}(M)$ respectively. The stabilization maps fit into the following commutative diagram of short exact sequence
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MCG}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{1}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MCG}_{1}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M)\oplus\mathbb{Z},L_{M})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{g}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MCG}_{g}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M)\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{g},L_{M})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}_{g+1}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MCG}_{g+1}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M)\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{g+1},L_{M})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\vdots}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}$
|
[S7.p1.7] where $\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M)\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{g},L_{M})\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M)\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{g+1},L_{M})$ is given by taking direct sum with $id:\mathbb{Z}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}$ . This is clearly injective for each $g$ . By the computation of $\mathcal{I}(M)$ we see that the stabilization map $\mathcal{I}_{g}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{g+1}(M)$ is injective for each $g$ , thus the stabilization map $\mathrm{MCG}_{g}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}_{g+1}(M)$ is injective for each $g$ . In other words, two orientation preserving diffeomorphisms of $M$ are not stably isotopic if they are not isotopic. This proves Corollary 1.7.
[S7.p2]
[S7.p2.1] In particular, the stabilization map for $M_{g}$ fits into the following commutative diagram
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g+1})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{GL}_{g+1}(\mathbb{Z}).}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}$
|
[S7.p2.2] By Lemma 6.7, $\phi_{*}:H_{1}(\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g}))\rightarrow H_{1}(\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z}))$ is an isomorphism for $g\geq 4$ . By the homological stability of $\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z})$ [Cha80, Theorem 3.2], the stabilization map induces an isomorphism $H_{1}(\mathrm{GL}_{g}(\mathbb{Z}))\cong H_{1}(\mathrm{GL}_{g+1}(\mathbb{Z}))$ for $g\geq 3$ . Thus, for $g\geq 4$ , the stabilization map induces an isomorphism
|
| $H_{1}(\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g}))\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{1}(\mathrm{MCG}(M_{g+1})).$
|
[S7.p2.3] This proves Corollary 1.8.
[S7.ThmDef1]
Remark 7.1.
[S7.ThmDef1.p1]
[S7.ThmDef1.p1.1] Let $W_{g}\coloneqq M_{g}\setminus\mathring{D}$ . Then one can define the stabilization map for relative diffeomorphism groups
|
| $\mathrm{Diff}(W_{g},\partial)\rightarrow\mathrm{Diff}(W_{g+1},\partial)$
|
[S7.ThmDef1.p1.2] which induces a stabilization map on the classifying spaces
|
| $B\mathrm{Diff}(W_{g},\partial)\rightarrow B\mathrm{Diff}(W_{g+1},\partial).$
|
[S7.ThmDef1.p1.3] The homological stability of $B\mathrm{Diff}(W_{g},\partial)$ under the stabilization map is an important topic in geometric topology (see for example [MW07, GRW18, GRW17, Per16a, Per16b, ER24]). For dimension 5 the homological stability result has not been established. Our result shows that $H_{1}(B\mathrm{Diff}(W_{g},\partial))$ stabilizes under the stabilization map when $g\geq 4$ .
[S7.p3]
[S7.p3.1] Finally we consider the colimits of the direct system of mapping class groups formed by the stabilization map. Denote
|
| $\displaystyle\mathrm{MCG}_{\infty}(M)$
| $\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{colim}_{g\to\infty}\mathrm{MCG}_{g}(M),$
|
|
| $\displaystyle\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(M)$
| $\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{colim}_{g\to\infty}\mathcal{I}_{g}(M),$
|
|
| $\displaystyle\mathrm{Aut}_{\infty}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})$
| $\displaystyle\coloneqq\mathrm{colim}_{g\to\infty}\mathrm{Aut}(H_{2}(M)\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{g},L_{M}).$
|
[S7.p3.2] We call $\mathrm{MCG}_{\infty}(M)$ the stable mapping class group of $M$ . Similarly $\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(M)$ is called the stable Torelli group of $M$ . The following result is evident.
[S7.ThmDef2]
Proposition 7.2.
[S7.ThmDef2.p1]
[S7.ThmDef2.p1.1] There is a short exact sequence
|
| $0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}_{\infty}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{Aut}_{\infty}(H_{2}(M),L_{M})\rightarrow 1.$
|
[S7.ThmDef2.p1.2] The stable Torelli group $\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(M)$ is an infinitely generated abelian group.
[S7.p4]
[S7.p4.1] For example, denote $\mathrm{MCG}_{\infty}(S^{5})$ and $\mathcal{I}_{\infty}(S^{5})$ by $\mathrm{MCG}_{\infty}$ and $\mathcal{I}_{\infty}$ respectively, then the short exact sequence takes the form
|
| $0\rightarrow\mathcal{I}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{MCG}_{\infty}\rightarrow\mathrm{GL}_{\infty}(\mathbb{Z})\rightarrow 1.$
|
[S7.p4.2] The stable Torelli group $\mathcal{I}_{\infty}=\mathrm{colim}_{g\to\infty}\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ is isomorphic to $\mathbb{Z}^{\infty}$ .
[S8]
8. Homotopy equivalences of $M$
[S8.p1]
[S8.p1.1] In this section $M$ denotes a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold with no 2-torsion in homology, unless otherwise specified. We compare diffeomorphisms and homotopy equivalences of $M$ using the forgetful homomorphism $\psi:\mathcal{I}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)$ . To formulate the main result of this section we need to recall some previous works.
[S8.p2]
[S8.p2.1] The homotopy Torelli group has a subgroup $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})$ consisting of elements which can be represented by homotopy equivalences restricting to the identity map on the complement of the interior of a fixed 5-cell. This group was completely computed by Baues and Buth in [BB96, Theorem B]. Moreover, in [BB96] Baues–Buth made an extensive investigation of the (unoriented) homotopy automorphism group of $M$ . In particular, in [BB96, page 581] they defined a homomorphism
|
| $t:\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)\rightarrow H_{6}(K(H_{2}(M),2)),$
|
[S8.p2.2] which is a homotopic version of the mapping torus construction $\rho$ defined in Section 2, and proved that (for $M$ satisfying our condition) the kernel of $t$ is $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})$ [BB96, Theorem D]. We denote the image of $t$ by $H_{M}^{h}$ .
[S8.p3]
[S8.p3.1] We also recall that Stöcker has proved that there is a bijection $\mathcal{S}(M)\rightarrow H_{3}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)$ [Stö82, Theorem 1.2], where $\mathcal{S}(M)$ is the surgery structure set of $M$ .
[S8.p4]
[S8.p4.1] From now on we suppose $H_{2}(M)$ has the isomorphism type of (5.3). For such $M$ the aforementioned results of Baues–Buth and Stöcker can be stated as follows.
[S8.ThmDef1]
Theorem 8.1 (Baues–Buth).
[S8.ThmDef1.p1]
[S8.ThmDef1.p1.1] There is a short exact sequence
|
| $0\rightarrow\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)\xrightarrow{t}H_{M}^{h}\rightarrow 0.$
|
[S8.ThmDef1.p1.2] If $H_{2}(M)$ has no 3-torsion, then $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2g}$ . If $H_{2}(M)$ has nontrivial 3-torsion, then $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2g}\oplus{(\mathbb{Z}/3)^{2s_{1}}}$ .
[S8.ThmDef2]
Theorem 8.2 (Stöcker).
[S8.ThmDef2.p1]
[S8.ThmDef2.p1.1] There is a bijection $\mathcal{S}(M)\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{g}$ .
[S8.ThmDef3]
Remark 8.3.
[S8.ThmDef3.p1]
[S8.ThmDef3.p1.1] For $M=M_{g}$ the existence of the bijection $\mathcal{S}(M_{g})\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{g}$ can be proved without using Stöcker’s work. By surgery exact sequence [Wal99] and the facts that $L_{5}(\mathbb{Z})=0$ and $\Theta_{5}=0$ , we have a bijection
|
| $\mathcal{S}(M)\cong[M,G/\mathrm{O}].$
|
[S8.ThmDef3.p1.2] The homotopy exact sequence of the homotopy fibration $G/\mathrm{O}\rightarrow B\mathrm{O}\rightarrow BG$ takes the form
|
| $\cdots\rightarrow\pi_{n}(\mathrm{O})\xrightarrow{J_{n}}\pi_{n}^{S}\rightarrow\pi_{n}(G/\mathrm{O})\rightarrow\pi_{n-1}(\mathrm{O})\rightarrow\cdots$
|
[S8.ThmDef3.p1.3] where $J_{n}:\pi_{n}(\mathrm{O})\rightarrow\pi_{n}^{S}$ is the stable $J$ -homomorphism. Using Adams’ work on $J_{n}$ [Ada66] and the fact that the Adams conjecture is true [Qui71], one could calculate the homotopy groups of $G/\mathrm{O}$ , and it follows from the Puppe sequence (and the CW structure of $M_{g}$ ) that there is a bijection
|
| $\mathcal{S}(M_{g})\cong[\vee_{g}S^{2},G/\mathrm{O}]\cong{(\pi_{2}(G/\mathrm{O}))}^{g}\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{g}.$
|
[S8.p5]
[S8.p5.1] Now we fix some notations. Our computation of $\mathcal{I}(M)$ shows that there is a surjective homomorphism $\mathcal{I}(M)\rightarrow H_{M}$ , given by $[f]\mapsto{\nu_{f}}_{*}([M_{f}])$ . By the definition of $t$ there is a commutative diagram
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{M}}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{M}^{h}.}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}$
|
[S8.p5.2] We denote the kernel of $\mathcal{I}(M)\rightarrow H_{M}$ by $\overline{K}(M)$ . According to 5.3, $\overline{K}(M)$ is generated by
|
| $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1}\#S_{1},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha},\;\cdots,\;\mathbf{t}_{S_{g+2(s_{1}+\cdots+s_{t})}\#S_{g+2(s_{1}+\cdots+s_{t})},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{g+2(s_{1}+\cdots+s_{t})},\alpha}.$
|
[S8.p5.3] When $H_{2}(M)$ has no 3-torsion, $\overline{K}(M)$ is isomorphic to $H_{2}(M)$ and these generators form a basis; when $H_{2}(M)$ has nontrivial 3-torsion, $\overline{K}(M)$ is isomorphic to $H_{2}(M)$ modulo a subgroup of its 3-primary subgroup. Let $K(M)$ be the subgroup of $\overline{K}(M)$ generated by
|
|
| $\displaystyle 2(\mathbf{t}_{S_{1}\#S_{1},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}),\;\cdots,\;2(\mathbf{t}_{S_{g}\#S_{g},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{g},\alpha}),$
|
|
|
| $\displaystyle\mathbf{t}_{S_{g+1}\#S_{g+1},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{g+1},\alpha},\;\cdots,\;\mathbf{t}_{S_{g+2(s_{1}+\cdots+s_{t})}\#S_{g+2(s_{1}+\cdots+s_{t})},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{g+2(s_{1}+\cdots+s_{t})},\alpha}.$
|
[S8.p5.4] It has the same isomorphism type as $\overline{K}(M)$ .
[S8.p6]
[S8.p6.1] The main result of this section is the following.
[S8.ThmDef4]
Theorem 8.4.
[S8.ThmDef4.p1]
[S8.ThmDef4.p1.1] Let $M$ be a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold with no 2- and 3-torsion in homology, then there is a commutative diagram of exact sequences (of based sets at $\mathcal{S}(M)$ , of groups at other terms)
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{K}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{M}}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{M}^{h}}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$
|
[S8.ThmDef4.p1.2] where the based map $\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(M)$ maps a homotopy class $[f]$ to the homotopy smoothing $[f:M\rightarrow M]$ .
[S8.p7]
[S8.p7.1] Theorem 1.9 follows from Theorem 8.4 as a special case. Before presenting the proof of Theorem 8.4 we elaborate this theorem by some remarks (in which $M$ is assumed to satisfy the conditions in Theorem 8.4).
[S8.ThmDef5]
Remark 8.5.
[S8.ThmDef5.p1]
[S8.ThmDef5.p1.1] By Theorem 8.4 $K(M)$ is the group of isotopy classes of diffeomorphisms of $M$ that are homotopic to $id_{M}$ , which is (abstractly) isomorphic to $H_{2}(M)$ and we have given explicit constructions of its generators. A result of Browder [Bro67, Theorem 6] implies that such group of a simply connected closed 5-manifold with positive second Betti number contains a free cyclic subgroup. Our computation of $K(M)$ can be viewed as a supplement of Browder’s result in dimension 5.
[S8.ThmDef6]
Remark 8.6.
[S8.ThmDef6.p1]
[S8.ThmDef6.p1.1] By our computation of $\mathcal{I}(M)$ , the left vertical short exact sequence in the diagram splits, thus so does the right vertical short exact sequence. Thus the homotopy Torelli group $\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)$ is a semi-direct product of $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})$ and $H_{M}$ . The action of $H_{M}$ on the elements of $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})$ that cannot be represented by diffeomorphisms is unknown, so the group structure of $\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)$ (for example, whether it is abelian) is unknown. For $M=S^{2}\times S^{3}$ , the homotopy Torelli group $\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ is not abelian ([Saw75], see Theorem 8.9).
[S8.ThmDef7]
Remark 8.7.
[S8.ThmDef7.p1]
[S8.ThmDef7.p1.1] According to Baues (see [BB96, page 580]) there is a surjective homomorphism
|
| $\pi_{5}(M)\rightarrow\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})$
|
[S8.ThmDef7.p1.2] mapping a homotopy class, represented by a map $\gamma:S^{5}\rightarrow M$ , to the homotopy class of the homotopy equivalence
|
| $M\rightarrow M\vee S^{5}\xrightarrow{id_{M}\vee{\gamma}}M,$
|
[S8.ThmDef7.p1.3] where $M\rightarrow M\vee S^{5}$ is the quotient map collapsing the boundary of the 5-cell to a point. In fact $\gamma$ can be chosen to factor through the 4-skeleton of $M$ . Combining with Theorem 8.4, this shows that each of the diffeomorphisms
|
| $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1}\#S_{1},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha},\;\cdots,\;\mathbf{t}_{S_{g}\#S_{g},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{g},\alpha}$
|
[S8.ThmDef7.p1.4] is homotopic to such a map. It is unknown that which homotopy classes do these diffeomorphisms correspond to (except for $S^{2}\times S^{3}$ , see below).
[S8.p8]
[S8.p8.1] Now we prove Theorem 8.4.
[S8.2]
Proof of Theorem 8.4.
[S8.p9]
[S8.p9.1] Clearly the diagram of the vertical short exact sequences commutes. By the definition of surgery structure set, the sequence
|
| $0\rightarrow\ker(\psi)\rightarrow\mathcal{I}(M)\xrightarrow{\psi}\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(M)$
|
[S8.p9.2] is exact, and the induced map $\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)/{\mathrm{im}(\psi)}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(M)$ is injective. This induces a commutative diagram of exact sequences (note that $\ker(\psi)$ is contained in $\overline{K}(M)$ since $H_{M}\rightarrow H_{M}^{h}$ is injective)
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\psi)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\overline{K}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker(\psi)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{I}^{h}(M)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{S}(M)}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\subset}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}$
|
[S8.p9.3] and the map $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})/{\mathrm{im}(\psi)}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(M)$ is also injective. By Baues–Buth’s result (Theorem 8.1) $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2g}$ , so the torsion subgroup of $\overline{K}(M)$ lies in $\ker(\psi)$ . Thus the upper horizontal exact sequence induces the following exact sequence
|
| $0\rightarrow\ker(\psi)/{\text{torsion}}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}^{g}\xrightarrow{\psi}{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2g}\rightarrow{(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{g}.$
|
[S8.p9.4] It follows that $\ker(\psi)/{\text{torsion}}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{g}$ , containing the isotopy classes of
|
| $2(\mathbf{t}_{S_{1}\#S_{1},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}),\;\cdots,\;2(\mathbf{t}_{S_{g}\#S_{g},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{g},\alpha}).$
|
[S8.p9.5] This implies that ${(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2g}/{\psi(\mathbb{Z}^{g})}$ has at least $2^{g}$ elements. Since ${(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{2g}/{\psi(\mathbb{Z}^{g})}$ admits an injection to ${(\mathbb{Z}/2)}^{g}$ , it contains precisely $2^{g}$ elements, and $\mathrm{haut}(M|\dot{M})\rightarrow\mathcal{S}(M)$ is surjective. Consequently we have $\ker(\psi)=K(M)$ , and $H_{M}$ is equal to $H_{M}^{h}$ . The proof is completed.
∎
[S8.ThmDef8]
Remark 8.8.
[S8.ThmDef8.p1]
[S8.ThmDef8.p1.1] If $M$ has nontrivial 3-torsion in homology, we still have the commutative diagram in the proof of Theorem 8.4. It follows that $\overline{K}(M)$ has nontrivial 3-torsion. For instance, if $H_{2}(M)\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/3)}^{2}$ , then $\overline{K}(M)$ is isomorphic to ${(\mathbb{Z}/3)}^{2}$ . This implies that the differential $d^{4}_{7,0}$ of the Atiyah–Hirzebruch spectral sequence of $\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{*}(K({(\mathbb{Z}/3)}^{2},2))$ is trivial. Consequently, we have $\Omega^{\mathrm{Spin}}_{7}(K({(\mathbb{Z}/3)}^{2},2))\cong H_{7}(K({(\mathbb{Z}/3)}^{2},2))\cong{(\mathbb{Z}/3)}^{4}$ .
[S8.p10]
[S8.p10.1] We close this section by discussing the case $M=S^{2}\times S^{3}$ , where we can use Sawashita’s work [Saw75] to obtain more concrete information about the group $\mathrm{haut}(S^{2}\times S^{3}|\dot{S^{2}\times S^{3}})$ . We will need the following result of Sawashita which is extracted from [Saw75, Proposition 2.4, Theorem 2.6, Lemma 4.1, Theorem 4.3].
[S8.ThmDef9]
Theorem 8.9 (Sawashita).
[S8.ThmDef9.p1]
[S8.ThmDef9.p1.1] Given a space $X$ , let $\mathcal{I}^{h}(X)$ be the group of homotopy classes of homotopy equivalences of $X$ that act trivially on $H_{*}(X)$ .
(1)
[S8.I1.ix1.p1]
[S8.I1.ix1.p1.1] There is an isomorphism
|
| $\pi_{3}(S^{2})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\vee S^{3})$
|
[S8.I1.ix1.p1.2] mapping a homotopy class $a[\eta]$ to the homotopy class of the homotopy equivalence
|
| $S^{2}\vee S^{3}\rightarrow S^{2}\vee S^{3}\vee S^{3}\xrightarrow{i_{1}\vee a\eta\vee j_{1}}S^{2}\vee S^{3},$
|
[S8.I1.ix1.p1.3] where $S^{2}\vee S^{3}\rightarrow S^{2}\vee S^{3}\vee S^{3}$ is the quotient map collapsing the equator of $S^{3}$ to a point, $i_{1}:S^{2}\hookrightarrow S^{2}\vee S^{3}$ and $j_{1}:S^{3}\hookrightarrow S^{2}\vee S^{3}$ are the inclusion maps, and $a\eta$ denotes a map representing the homotopy class $a[\eta]$ .
(2)
[S8.I1.ix2.p1]
[S8.I1.ix2.p1.1] Identify $S^{2}\vee S^{3}$ with the 4-skeleton of $S^{2}\times S^{3}$ . Then there is a splitting short exact sequence
|
| $0\rightarrow\pi_{5}(S^{2}\times S^{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\times S^{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\vee S^{3})\rightarrow 0$
|
[S8.I1.ix2.p1.2] where $\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\times S^{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\vee S^{3})$ is induced by restriction on the 4-skeleton, and $\pi_{5}(S^{2}\times S^{3})\rightarrow\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ is the homomorphism defined in Remark 8.7. The action of $\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\vee S^{3})$ on $\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ is as follows: identify $\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\vee S^{3})$ with $\pi_{3}(S^{2})$ via the isomorphism in (1), let $a[\eta]\in\pi_{3}(S^{2})$ and $([x],[y])\in\pi_{5}(S^{2})\oplus\pi_{5}(S^{3})=\pi_{5}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ be homotopy classes, then
|
| $a[\eta]\cdot([x],[y])=([x]+[(a\eta)\circ y],[y]).$
|
[S8.p11]
[S8.p11.1] Combining Theorem 8.9 with Baues’ result stated in Remark 8.7, we see that $\pi_{5}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ is isomorphic to $\mathrm{haut}(S^{2}\times S^{3}|\dot{S^{2}\times S^{3}})$ . It follows from Theorem 8.4 that $\mathcal{I}^{h}(S^{2}\vee S^{3})$ is generated by the restriction of $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}$ on $S^{2}\vee S^{3}$ (this can also be seen from the effect of $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}$ on $\pi_{3}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ ), and $\mathrm{haut}(S^{2}\times S^{3}|\dot{S^{2}\times S^{3}})$ contains $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1}\#S_{1},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}$ . Since $\mathcal{I}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ is abelian, $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}$ acts on $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1}\#S_{1},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}$ trivially, thus $\mathbf{t}_{S_{1}\#S_{1},\alpha}-8\mathbf{t}_{S_{1},\alpha}$ corresponds to the generator of $\pi_{5}(S^{2})$ via the isomorphism
|
| $\pi_{5}(S^{2})\oplus\pi_{5}(S^{3})=\pi_{5}(S^{2}\times S^{3})\cong\mathrm{haut}(S^{2}\times S^{3}|\dot{S^{2}\times S^{3}}).$
|
[S8.p11.2] It follows that the homotopy equivalences defined by the homotopy classes $(0,1),(1,1)\in\pi_{5}(S^{2})\oplus\pi_{5}(S^{3})=\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2$ are not homotopic to any diffeomorphism of $S^{2}\times S^{3}$ .
[S9]
9. Embeddings of $S^{3}$ in $S^{2}\times S^{3}$
[S9.p1]
[S9.p1.1] In this section we prove Theorem 1.10 and Corollary 1.11.
[S9.2]
Proof of Theorem 1.10.
[S9.p2]
[S9.p2.1] In Section 4 we determined the action of $\mathcal{I}(M_{g})$ on $\pi_{3}(M_{g})$ . For $g=1$ , combining this with the computation of $\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ in 6.3, we see that a homotopy class $a[S_{1}\circ\eta]+b[T_{1}]\in\pi_{3}(S^{2}\times S^{3})=\mathbb{Z}[S_{1}\circ\eta]\oplus\mathbb{Z}[T_{1}]$ (denoted by $(a,b)$ for simplicity) can be represented by $f\circ T_{1}$ for some diffeomorphism $f\in\mathrm{Diff}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ if and only if $\left|b\right|=1$ . This proves (1).
[S9.p3]
[S9.p3.1] Now we prove (2). We shall prove a slightly more general result relating embeddings of $S^{3}$ in $S^{2}\times S^{3}$ to closed 5-manifolds with certain homology. Assume a homotopy class $(a,b)$ can be represented by an embedding of $S^{3}$ , where $b\neq 0$ . Since $\pi_{2}(\mathrm{SO}(2))=0$ , the normal bundle of this embedding is trivial, so there is an embedding $T^{\prime}:S^{3}\times D^{2}\hookrightarrow S^{2}\times S^{3}$ extending it. (This extension is in fact unique up to isotopy since $\pi_{3}(\mathrm{SO}(2))=0$ .) We perform a surgery on $T^{\prime}$ and denote the resulting closed spin 5-manifold by $M^{\prime}$ . Using Seifert–van Kampen theorem, Lefschetz duality and homology exact sequence of pairs, we see that $\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})\cong\mathbb{Z}/{\left|b\right|}$ and the homology groups of $M^{\prime}$ are given as follows.
[S9.tab1.1.1]
$i$
$0$
$1$
$2$
$3$
$4$
$5$
[S9.tab1.1.2]
$H_{i}(M^{\prime})$
| $\mathbb{Z}$
| $\mathbb{Z}/{\left|b\right|}$
| $0$
| $\mathbb{Z}/{\left|b\right|}$
| $0$
| $\mathbb{Z}$
[S9.p4]
[S9.p4.1] Conversely, let $M^{\prime}$ be a closed spin 5-manifold with such homology and fundamental group. Choose a generator of $\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})$ , it can be represented by an embedding $S^{1}\times D^{4}\hookrightarrow M^{\prime}$ , and we perform a surgery on this embedding. The resulting manifold is a simply connected closed spin 5-manifold with homology isomorphic to $H_{*}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ , so by the classification of simply connected 5-manifolds [Sma62, Bar65] it is diffeomorphic to $S^{2}\times S^{3}$ . The meridian sphere of this surgery represents an element of $\pi_{3}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ , which is $(a,b)$ or $(a,-b)$ for some $a\in\mathbb{Z}$ . By the computation of $\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ , the homotopy classes $(a+kb,b)$ and $(a+kb,-b)$ can be represented by embeddings of $S^{3}$ for each integer $k$ . For $\left|b\right|=2$ such a manifold is provided by the manifold $Q_{0}$ constructed in [GT98, page 1168], and for $\left|b\right|\geq 3$ odd such a manifold is provided by a 5-dimensional lens space $L_{\left|b\right|}(\ell_{1},\ell_{2},\ell_{3})$ (see for example [Hat02, Example 2.43]). This proves (2).
∎
[S9.ThmDef1]
Remark 9.1.
[S9.ThmDef1.p1]
[S9.ThmDef1.p1.1] By a theorem of Whitney (see for example [Ran02, page 169]), each element of $\pi_{3}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ can be represented by an immersion of $S^{3}$ .
[S9.3]
Proof of Corollary 1.11.
[S9.p5]
[S9.p5.1] For any diffeomorphism $f\in\mathrm{Diff}(S^{2}\times S^{3})$ , the absolute value of the coefficient of the $[T_{1}]$ -factor of $[f\circ T^{\prime}]$ is the same as that of $[T^{\prime}]$ . This shows that the map
|
| $\mathrm{MCG}^{\pm}(S^{2}\times S^{3})=\pi_{0}\mathrm{Diff}(S^{2}\times S^{3})\rightarrow\pi_{0}\mathrm{Emb}(S^{3},S^{2}\times S^{3})$
|
[S9.p5.2] induced by the restriction map $f\mapsto f\circ T^{\prime}$ is not surjective.
∎
[A1]
Appendix A A direct proof of the claim that $\theta(W,\nu)$ is elementary
[A1.p1]
[A1.p1.1] We use the notations in the proof of Lemma 5.2. Write $N_{0}={\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{3})\times\{0\}$ and $N_{1}={\natural}^{g}(S^{2}\times D^{3})\times\{1\}$ . By [Kre99, Corollary 1] we may assume that the normal $B$ -structure $\nu:W\rightarrow B$ is 3-connected. Now the obstruction $\theta(W,\nu)$ is represented by the following data
|
| $\Bigl(H_{3}(W,N_{0})\leftarrow\mathrm{im}(d:\pi_{4}(B,W)\rightarrow\pi_{3}(W))\rightarrow H_{3}(W,N_{1}),\lambda,\tilde{\mu}\Bigr)$
|
[A1.p1.2] where $d$ is the connecting homomorphism in the homotopy exact sequence of $\nu:W\rightarrow B$ , and $\mathrm{im}(d:\pi_{4}(B,W)\rightarrow\pi_{3}(W))\rightarrow H_{3}(W,N_{i})$ is given by $\pi_{3}(W)\rightarrow\pi_{3}(W,N_{i})\rightarrow H_{3}(W,N_{i})$ . Using this exact sequence and the fact that $\nu:W\rightarrow B$ is 3-connected, we deduce that $\mathrm{im}(d:\pi_{4}(B,W)\rightarrow\pi_{3}(W))=\pi_{3}(W)$ and there is a commutative diagram
|
| ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{3}(W)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{3}(W,N_{i})}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(W)}$ ${\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(W,N_{i})}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}$ $\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}$
|
[A1.p1.3] where the vertical maps are Hurewicz homomorphisms. Thus
|
| $\theta(W,\nu)=\Bigl(H_{3}(W)\leftarrow\pi_{3}(W)\rightarrow H_{3}(W),\lambda\Bigr)$
|
[A1.p1.4] where $\lambda$ is the intersection form on $H_{3}(W)$ and the maps are Hurewicz homomorphisms ( $\tilde{\mu}=0$ since it maps into $\mathbb{Z}/1=0$ by the definition of $l_{6}^{\sim}(e)$ ). Viewing $W$ as a simply connected compact 6-manifold with boundary $M_{g}$ , we deduce from the homology exact sequence of $(W,M_{g})$ that $H_{3}(W)$ is free. The Hurewicz homomorphism $\pi_{3}(W)\rightarrow H_{3}(W)$ is surjective (which can be seen from, for example, the homotopy exact sequence of $(W,N_{i})$ ), thus $H_{3}(W)$ is a direct summand of $\pi_{3}(W)$ . By Poincaré duality, $\lambda$ is a skew-symmetric non-singular bilinear form on $H_{3}(W)$ , thus $H_{3}(W)$ has a symplectic basis $(e_{1},\cdots,e_{n},f_{1},\cdots,f_{n})$ , such that the nonzero values of $\lambda$ on them are
|
| $\lambda(e_{i},f_{i})=1,\quad\lambda(f_{i},e_{i})=-1,\quad i=1,\cdots,n.$
|
[A1.p1.5] The rank $n$ free abelian group $U$ generated by $e_{1},\cdots,e_{n}$ is a direct summand of $H_{3}(W)$ . View $U$ as a direct summand of $\pi_{3}(W)$ and let $U_{0}=U_{1}$ be the image of $U$ under the Hurewicz homomorphism $\pi_{3}(W)\rightarrow H_{3}(W)$ . One checks that $U$ and $U_{i}$ satisfy the conditions in the definition in [Kre99, Page 730], proving that $\theta(W,\nu)$ is elementary.

We are continuing to improve HTML versions of papers, and your feedback helps enhance accessibility and mobile
support. To report errors in the HTML that will help us improve conversion and rendering, choose any of the
methods listed below:
Click the "Report Issue" (
) button, located in the page header.
Tip: You can select the relevant text first, to include it in your report.
Our team has already identified the following issues. We appreciate your time reviewing and reporting rendering errors we
may not have found yet. Your efforts will help us improve the HTML versions for all readers, because disability
should not be a barrier to accessing research. Thank you for your continued support in championing open access for
all.
